XP is kept once, not copied onto every member: a copy per item needed every writer to reach every member and drifted as soon as a member had no row. A weapon the catalogue does not list is its own key.
- **A hit** (permitted ammunition, as before: 2 XP, half for a ground bird) adds to the highest XP any item of the family holds and leaves the one row under the representative. The level-up toast names the item that was fired.
- **Skill/list** and the connect's `skills` (levels) give the family's number to every member item of every family the player has a row in, so the game shows one level whichever variant is equipped. The website's and launcher's Skills tab (`GET /hunters/<handle>/skills`, [Public reads](#public-reads)) has one row per family, named by its representative (by the first shown member when the representative is hidden, below), with the other finishes listed under it. The tab lists a weapon item, as a row or as a finish, only if the store catalogue lists it or the account owns it (an item row in its inventory): an item that only the game's install data defines (restored accounts hold a minimal row on a few) and nobody owns is shown nowhere and never names a row (a family with no shown member is not listed, and the row is named by the first shown member when the representative is hidden). A family the account has a stored row for is listed when one of its members is shown, wherever the row is stored (per item before the merge, under the representative after it, or after a hit): the visibility rule decides, not the storage form, so the merge never adds or removes a card. A family none of whose members is shown (an install-only weapon alone that nobody owns) is not listed, and neither is a row for a weapon the catalogue lacks and the account does not own. Storage, `Skill/list` and the merge are not affected: the family's one value stays, and the game shows it on every member.
- **A new account, a restore and the claim of an old profile** fold the weapon levels they carry the same way: the variants of one weapon end with the highest of the restored levels, under the representative.
- **Rows saved per item** (every account before this rule) read as the highest of their family at once, so no reader shows a lowered skill before the merge, and the first hit folds the family. `admin weapon-skills` folds them all at once.

`admin weapon-skills [--apply] [--account <user id or handle>]` (#1068) puts every account's weapon skill rows in the one-row form: a family's rows become one row under its representative with the highest XP any of them holds, so a skill is never lowered and a weapon alone is not touched; species skills are not read. Without `--apply` it writes nothing and prints, in a stable order: one line per account (ascending) and family (ascending by representative) that changes, with the rows before and after, `account 7 family 5 (Name): before 5=4.1 xp (number 1.273), 706=12.1 xp (number 1.807); after 5=12.1 xp (number 1.807)`; one line per family: `family 5 (Name): N account(s) change, M with a row that rises, K of them on an item the Skills tab shows, largest rise X xp, total Y xp` (N counts every account whose rows change, even when they only move to the representative's key; M those where a member's XP rises to the highest; K those where such a row is on an item the tab shows by the rule above, so rises that are only on install-only items nobody owns stand apart; the rise is the highest minus the lowest row of that account and family); then `N account(s) to change, F family(ies)` and the dry-run hint. Each account's lines are printed as soon as that account is done (committed, with `--apply`), the family lines and the total at the end, so an interrupted apply leaves the record of the accounts already changed. `--apply` writes each account in one transaction and ends with `N account(s) changed`; a second run changes nothing (`0 account(s)`). It never runs at start. Like the other one-time steps, run it with the server stopped, after `backup`, and rehearse it on a copy of the live database first: read the dry run before applying. It reads the catalogue and install definitions as the server does, so the families are the ones the running server uses.

## Weapons of a mission

An objective that names a weapon accepts every member of that weapon's [family](#weapon-families), so the engraved twin of a bow counts for the base bow's objective and the event finish of a handgun for the objective of its plain one ([Conditions of a harvest](#missions)). The rest is unchanged: an objective that names one finish (the operator's `weapon: Name (Finish)`, [Overridden rules](#missions); no title of the reader names a finish) stays that item; a class (`any bow`, `any handgun`) is the class's weapons, and an event finish filed under an event sub-category (any sub-category that is none of the five classes: rifles, shotguns, handguns, muzzleloaders, bows and crossbows) takes the class of the standard finish of its family (a weapon of an event sub-category with no such family mate is a rifle or a shotgun by the last word of its name); and the ammunition, `permitted`, distance and every other condition apply to the hit whatever family member fired it. A family member that fits an ammunition the named weapon does not (an event finish with the event's silver ammunition) is accepted as the weapon; its kill still needs the ammunition the objective names and, with `permitted`, a permitted one. Only the catalogue's weapons count (as in the reader's classes): a weapon only the install defines is no part of an objective.

The shorter-name rule stays: a name whose words all lie in a longer name also accepts that longer name's weapons (`Test Bow` and `Heavy Test Bow`), which no family relates. A weapon with a family of its own (more than one item) takes only a longer name that holds its name whole, not one that has other words between its words (`Test Bolt Rifle` is not the `Test Maker Bolt Rifle`).

Other readings of the mission fixes (the invented examples are in `server/Server.Tests/mission-cases`):

- A bare `bow` also takes the longbow; `any pistol or revolver` is any handgun; `any ethical rifle ammunition` and `any muzzleloader ammunition` are the ammunition of the catalogue's sub-categories whose name spells that class (the word `rifle`, or a word that starts with `muzzle`; the muzzleloaders' rounds are rifle ammunition too; no weapon condition).
- An ammunition phrase is read as the phrase, not as its calibre alone: `.30-30` is not `.30-06`. Tracer arrows include an arrow whose variation names a tracer.
- `one heart shot` is one shot, through the heart. `without using a Waterfowl Blind` accepts every stand but that one; `without using a stand` (a bare word) still refuses every stand.
- A title with a gender word (`rooster`) in a mission of one species is that species.
- A `Spot another ...`, `a second/third ...`, `one last/final ...` objective needs the n-th different animal of the mission, as a clue does; `unique` is read as `another`. A clue or spot counts its animal by the spawn's individual.
- `Harvest a ... first.` makes the next objective ordered; `After visiting X, ...` waits only for the arrive objective that names X (printed `after objective N`).
- `At any point during the same hunt, ...` (also `At any time during the same hunt`) in a mission with a `Start at ...` objective before it waits for that objective (printed `after objective N`); no other `At any ...` wording does. In a mission played in one hunt (`singleExpedition`) the start is forgotten at every new hunt and met again only by the lodge a hunt starts at, so the objective ticks only in the hunt that started at the lodge; the reader does not check the hunt itself, so in a mission of several hunts the start, once met, stays met and the objective ticks in a later hunt too. An operator rule for such an objective keeps this order.
- Weapons of two classes: the muzzleloader rifles are rifles too; a weapon of a sub-category whose name spells two classes (the words `muzzle...` and `pistol`: a muzzleloading pistol) is a muzzleloader and a handgun; a crossbow whose name has the word `pistol` is a crossbow and a handgun; a weapon of a sub-category that is none of the five classes whose name starts with cartridges joined by slashes (`10GA/8x50R Test Combination Gun`) is of each class whose ammunition fits one of them (the ammunition is found by the cartridge's words in its name, its class by the words of its sub-category: `rifle`, `shotgun`, `muzzle...`) and fires each class only with that class's ammunition (only where the title names the class; named by name, it is its whole family).
- A title that names ammunition without `permitted` gets the `permitted` condition when its animal (the title's species, else the mission's) permits one of the rounds named; otherwise it stays as the title says (the objective would be impossible).
- A bare `any clue` takes every kind of clue (rooting and water bubbles too); `ID 3 tracks from a <species>` (a count with no `same animal`) is three clues of any animals of the species in the hunt (`in the hunt: 3`); `the same <animal>` and `Locate ... from the same animal` stay one animal.
- `platform` is a raised stand only (tower, tree stand, tripod stand); `blind` is any blind; so `without using a platform` accepts blinds and the tripod rest, `without using a platform or blind` accepts the tripod rest.
- `more than N typical points` counts N (`less than` stays strict). An animal state (`spooked`, `charging`, `airborne`, ...) is the killing shot's; `unspooked` (unaware when first shot) is the first hit's.
- An operator's rule `named animal near (x, z) m, N animals, otherwise place DEFINE` is a mission's own group of N animals (the placement of `spawn_named_animals`, one population): with the setting on only an animal of the group meets it, wherever it is shot and wherever the hunter stands; with it off (the default) it is met by an animal shot within 500 m of the point or by a hunter in the place `DEFINE` at the killing shot. `, N animals` and `, otherwise place` are each optional. The hunt's reply holds the same `spawn` entry as for one animal (one population, one group record at the point) with `male_count` N (`female_count` N for a female rule): the placement of single animals has been tested in the game, a group of several animals has not been seen yet.
- An operator's rule may say `together` (`harvest; species: ...; together`): one kill that meets it finishes every unfinished objective of the mission whose rule says the same words, not only the first (a mission whose objectives all ask for the one animal).
- An operator's track rule may say `footprints of each of <species>, <species> within <R> m of (<x>, <z>)`: the objective is finished by a clue of footprints of one of the species within R metres of the point once every listed species has left such a footprint clue near the point since the mission was activated (any hunt, any reserve); one species alone, or footprints farther away, do not finish it. It is written with `track; clues: footprints x1; ...`.

**The exact list.** `coverage --rules` prints it under `weapons:` ([Reading the rules](#missions)). `admin mission-weapons <folder>` writes the two audit tables of the catalogue the server loaded (a community server: `offline_mode` false with a recording) into the folder, Markdown, the same bytes for the same catalogue:

- `mission-objectives.md`: every objective of every mission (the catalogue's, then the daily missions' and the running event's community missions, as `daily <id>` and `community <id>`), ascending: mission, objective, title, the weapon condition (`none`, `class`, `named weapon`, `named weapon + class`, `any weapon but`, `named finish`) and the accepted weapons (`<id> <name> (<finish>)`), then a count line. An operator's entry is read from its words, so a list it gives is `named weapon` (or `named finish`) even when it was copied from a class.
- `weapon-missions.md`: every weapon item of the catalogue by id, its name, finish and family (the representative's id), and the objectives that accept it (`<mission>/<objective>`), then a count line (the weapons no objective accepts: `none`).

Like `admin weapon-families` they list the catalogue's names, which are the operator's data ([Starting data](#starting-data)): the repository does not commit them. `MissionWeaponTests` pins their shape and stability on an invented catalogue, and each kind of family with it.

**On the missions page** `GET /account/missions` (the website's and the launcher's) has beside `objectives` the list `objective_weapons`: one list per objective, one name per weapon (`Name`, or `Name (Finish, Finish)` when only some finishes of that name count), sorted, empty for an objective that names no weapon (a class, any weapon, none). The page shows it under the objective as a collapsed "Weapons that count:" disclosure that opens one line per weapon. The in-game missions panel is the game's own and gets nothing new: its reply is the game's strict format, and the client has no place to draw a list.

## The launch map

`GET /catalog/reserves` on `web_host`, community server only (a local server answers the miss reply), public and without a cookie, from the application catalogue the server loaded ([Starting data](#starting-data)): the reserves and start places of the facts file, and the catalogue's tutorials. The launcher's map reads it. The answer is `{reserves: [{id, name, starts: [{id, kind, name, x, y}]}], tutorials: [{id, name, reserve}]}`; one with no reserve answers 404 `{"error": "no reserves"}`.

- **Reserves** are the catalogue's, in its order (the 12 of the facts file). A reserve with no start has an empty `starts`.
- **Starts** are the catalogue's `locations` of the types `LODGE`, `CAMPSITE`, `HUNTING_TOWER` and `INTEREST`, by id; `kind` is `lodge`, `campsite`, `tower` or `interest`. `id` is the location id, the number `POST /auth/launch` takes as `lodge`. A start's name is the location's `name`, else its `name` in the connect's `locations_descr` (the texts the game shows by location id), else the kind and its number in the reserve (`Lodge 1`, `Tower 3`). The hunt history uses the same names: the website's rows and hunt page show the name of the start the hunt opened at, and the game's `Expedition/list` carries its `location_id`, which the game resolves through the same `locations_descr`.
- **Position**: `x` and `y` are percent of the reserve's map picture (`map-<NN>`, [images.md](images.md)), 0 to 100 from the left and from the top, with two decimals. The picture covers the box of the facts file's `reserves[].map` (world `x` and `z` of its left, top, right and bottom edges), world `x` runs to the right and world `z` down, so a start at world (`x`, `z`) is at `(x - left) / (right - left)` and `(z - top) / (bottom - top)`, times 100. A start outside the box gets a value below 0 or above 100 (the map does not show it). A reserve with no usable `map` (missing, not four numbers, or an empty box) has the square around its starts instead: its side is the larger spread of their world `x` and `z`, plus a quarter of that for a tenth of margin on each side, centred on the middle of the starts, and a reserve whose starts all stand in one place has them at 50, 50; the picture is not cut to that square, so the starts are right relative to each other only.
- **Tutorials**: the catalogue's `id`, `name` and the `reserve` the tutorial runs in.
- **Placed items**: `GET /account/placed` on `web_host`, community server only, with the session cookie, lists the signed-in player's own placed items the map shows (401 when signed out; another player's are never listed): `{items: [{id, reserve, kind, name, x, y}]}`, one for each item in slot 23 ([Placed items and stacks](#placed-items-and-stacks)) that has a position and a reserve and whose catalogue `define` is a tent (`TENT`, `TENT02` ...), an electronic caller, a ground or waterfowl blind, a tree stand, a tripod stand, a deployable hunting tower or climbing gear (`CLIMBING_GEAR`, the ropes of the cliffs). `id` is the item's guid, `reserve` the web's reserve number, `kind` `tent`, `caller`, `groundblind`, `treestand`, `tripod`, `deployabletower` or `climbing` (from the define, never from the name), `name` the catalogue's name (else `Tent`, `Caller`, `Blind`, `Stand`, `Tripod`, `Tower` or `Rope`), `x` and `y` percent of the reserve's map picture as for a start, from the item's position. Other placed items (feeders, decoys, targets) are not listed. The launcher draws each kind with its own picture ([images.md](images.md)).

`GET /account/loadouts` (below) lists the player's saved loadouts for the picker.

### A hunt at a tent

A placed tent is a permanent start of its player, a place to begin a hunt next to the lodges. The original's launch map listed the player's tents with the lodges, and picking one sent `Launch/start` `{start, loctype, reserve, time, loadout_id}` as for a lodge, with `start` the tent's item id (its guid), `loctype` the item's own `define` (`TENT`, `TENT06` ... where a lodge sends `LODGE`) and `reserve` the reserve it stands in. The game needs no other field to begin there: the connect reply's `expedition` holds the spawn pose, `x`, `y`, `z` in metres (the game uses it when `x` and `z` are both not 0) and `dir_x`, `dir_y`, `dir_z` in per mille, and the game reads its reserve from `lodge` as `lodge / 100`. Before schema 21 this server handled no start but a lodge's location number.

- **Launch**: `POST /auth/launch` takes `tent` (a guid of [`GET /account/placed`](#the-launch-map)) in place of `lodge`, and `Launch/start` takes a tent's define as `loctype` with the guid as `start`. The server checks the item, not the digits of the `loctype`: it must be the account's own, a tent (its `define` is `TENT`, then digits, as in `TENT06`; the heated tents are `TENT` and a number too), in slot 23 and placed in `reserve`. `POST /auth/launch` answers 400 and opens nothing for any other item, a tent of another reserve, a tent that is not placed, and for both a `lodge` and a `tent`; `Launch/start` opens the hunt with no start, as it does for any start that is nothing.
- **Record**: the hunt keeps the tent's guid (`expeditions.tent`, schema 21) and its location is the reserve's first location number, `(reserve - 1) * 100`, because the game reads its reserve from it; the hunt history names that first start of the reserve. No `Start at` objective of a lodge is met at a tent.
- **Connect**: the reply's `expedition` has `x`, `y`, `z` the tent's position (metres, the row's millimetres divided by 1000), `dir_x`, `dir_y`, `dir_z` its direction (the row's, already per mille) and `lodge` that location number. A tent picked up after the launch is no start any more: the connect then answers the location's pose, as for a hunt from the catalogue's start.

## Multiplayer

The game plays multiplayer peer to peer: the joiners connect straight to the host's PC over UDP, and animals, shots, chat and kicks run between the games. The server never relays any of it. It creates the session, says in each player's connect reply who the host is and where it is, learns the host's port and network id from the heartbeat, and lists the open sessions. Each player keeps their own heartbeat, so kills, scores and missions stay per player. A session lives in memory (`Games` in `Multiplayer.cs`) and ends with its host; there is no table and no schema change. Community server only: a local server has none.

A session also keeps the **language** its host chose for it (`Games.Languages`, the launcher's 19 interface language codes such as `fr` and `pt-BR`; `Games.LanguageOf` accepts any case and gives the code as the launcher spells it, and none for a missing or unknown code, which the game's own client, an older launcher and a code the server does not have all are). It is kept with the session in memory like the rest (no table, no schema change) and listed as `language` by `GET /catalog/games` (null for none). A language is only a label for the players choosing a game: it limits nobody from joining.

| Call | Host | Answer |
|---|---|---|
| `POST /v1/Launch/start_mp` | API | The caller hosts a session and a hunt opens as at `Launch/start`: `start` and `loctype` (a lodge, or a tent's define with its guid as `start`), `reserve`, `time`, `loadout_id`, and the session's `name` (up to 25 characters; default `<handle>'s game`), `description` (255), `language` (one of the launcher's 19 language codes, `fr` or `pt-BR`, in any case; the game's own client sends none), `public_game` (0: friends only, otherwise anyone), `free_players`, `hs_min`, `hs_max` (a hunterscore range, at most 99999), `password` (20; none is open), `max_players` (2 to 8; default 8). The reply is `Launch/start`'s: `{access_token, refresh_token, session, status: "ok"}`. |
| `POST /v1/Launch/join` | API | The caller joins the session `network_id` of the list: `start`, `loctype`, `loadout_id`, `password`, and `time` (default: the host's). The hunt opens in the session's reserve. The reply is `Launch/start`'s, or with HTTP 200 `{"errorCode": 23, "message": "USER_CANT_JOIN"}` for a wrong password, a full session (the host counts), a session whose host banned the caller ([The host's bans](#the-hosts-bans)), a friends-only session of a player who is not the host's friend, a hunterscore outside the range, the host's own session (`own_game` at `/auth/launch`), an unknown or ended `network_id`, and a session whose host has not reported its port yet. |
| `POST /v1/User/ban_player`, form `oauth_access_token`, `banned_user_id` | API | The host's Ban button: the caller bans that player from its games, once ([The host's bans](#the-hosts-bans)). `true`; `{"errorCode": 400}` for the caller's own id and any id that is not a player of its multiplayer session or one it may see ([Public reads](#public-reads)). The game's page ignores the reply. |
| `GET` or `POST /v1/Network/list` | API | No token (the original refuses one); the same list is `GET /catalog/games` on `web_host`, the one the reverse proxy reaches, for a signed-in player only (the `hr_session` cookie; 401 `signed_out` without a session). The open sessions, oldest first, as the original lists them: `[{id, name, handle, host, description, password: bool, public, singleplayer: false, freeplayers, hs_min, hs_max, max_players, start_ts, clients_len, clients: [{user_id, handle, guid, color, avatar, x, y, z, hunterscore}], reserve: {id, define, name, species, border}}]`. `id` is the `network_id` of `Launch/join`. `guid` is the member's network id as decimal text and `color` its `rrggbb`, both empty until its heartbeat event 29; `x`, `y`, `z` are the host's `player_positions`, 0 until the first, and 0 for the players of a friends-only or password session (the list cannot tell who asks; `GET /catalog/games` shows them to a caller who may join). There are no `country_code` and `continent_code`, `species` and `border` are empty, and `description` is null when empty. `GET /catalog/games` also gives each game `language`, the code its host chose (null for none), and `distance` ([How far a host is](#how-far-a-host-is)); the game's own list has neither. |
| `GET /stats/game` | web | Public like `/stats/online`. `{"build": n}`, the `game_build` setting (`null` when unset), sent with `Cache-Control: public, max-age=300`. The launcher compares it with the installed game's build id. |
| `GET /stats/online` | web | No token, no cookie; the reverse proxy reaches it ([deploy.md](deploy.md#the-community-server)). `{"players": n, "games": m}`, counts only, never a handle: `players` the accounts with a hunt open and a heartbeat in the last `HEARTBEAT_TIMEOUT` (120 s; a banned account and the recorded player's are not counted, as for the `online` of [Public reads](#public-reads)), `games` the open sessions of `Network/list`. The server counts at most once in 30 s and sends `Cache-Control: public, max-age=30`. It reads each account's hunts on its own connection (`State.OnlineCounts`), so a count costs one query per account. |
| `GET /stats/hunting` | web | For a signed-in player (the `hr_session` cookie; 401 `signed_out` without one). `{reserves: [{reserve, hours, gmPerHour, gmPerHarvest, comparableGmPerHour, comparableGmPerHarvest, harvestsPerHour, players}], species: [{species, hours, gmPerHour, gmPerHarvest, comparableGmPerHour, comparableGmPerHarvest, harvestsPerHour, players, bestReserve}], since, events: [{name, kind, start, end, multiplier}]}`: the server-wide hunting statistics, never a player's name, handle or rank, a row of fewer than 5 players left out ([Server playtime and hunting statistics](#server-playtime-and-hunting-statistics)). `gmPerHour` and `gmPerHarvest` are as paid; the `comparable` ones use each payment's base, and `events` are the events that overlap the period (from `since` until now): when it is not empty, the paid figures include their bonuses. Kept an hour |
| `POST /v1/Map/locations` | API | `[{id, define, name, description, reserve, type, pos, metadata}]`: the catalogue's locations of the reserve of the session `network_id` (else of `reserve`; `[]` for neither). The world items placed in the session are not listed. |
| `POST /auth/launch` with `mp` | web | The same two calls for the launcher and the website's start page, see [Sign-in and tokens](#sign-in-and-tokens). |

**Who may join.** `Games.Why` is the one rule of whether a player may join a session: the first of `own` (the player hosts it), `banned` (the host banned the player from its games, [The host's bans](#the-hosts-bans)), `friends` (friends only and the host is not the player's friend), `hunterscore` (the player's hunterscore is outside the session's range), `starting` (the host's port and network id are not known yet), `network` (the host's recorded address is IPv6, which the game cannot use; an IPv4-mapped IPv6 address counts as IPv4), `password` (the session has one and none, or a wrong one, was given), `full`; null when the player may. `Launch/join` refuses with it (`own_game`, `banned`, `not_allowed` for `friends` and `hunterscore`, `host_not_ready`, `host_network` for `network`, `password`, `full`), and `GET /catalog/games` adds to each session, for the signed-in caller, `joinable` (true when `why` is null) and `why`, so the list and the join never disagree. A session with a password is never `joinable`: its `why` is `password`.

**The host's bans.** The in-game Ban button of the players page posts `User/ban_player` over http to the API host with the host's `oauth_access_token` and the `banned_user_id` of the row, ignores the reply and kicks the player itself; the server also drops him from the host's running session at once (he no longer counts toward the players and his session token no longer connects). The server keeps the pair and the time in `host_bans`: the ban is the host's own list ("from your games"), never a ban from the server, and only that host lifts it. A second ban of the same player changes nothing (the first time stays). The host's own id, an unknown id and any id the host may not name (neither a player of its multiplayer session nor one it may see) get `{"errorCode": 400}` and store nothing, so walking user ids lists no handle in the website's list. `Games.Why` answers `banned` to a player the host of the session banned, right after `own`, so the join is refused, the list shows that session as not joinable with `why` `banned`, and its positions and the host's placed items (`GET /catalog/games/<id>/placed`, 403) stay hidden from him like for any other refusal; every other player is unaffected, and so is every game of another host. The website's and the launcher's **Settings → Multiplayer bans** lists the bans (`GET /account/bans`) and lifts one (`POST /account/bans/unban`); the game's own `User/unban_player` is not answered.

**Positions in the list.** The host's heartbeat carries `player_positions` (`user_id`, `x`, `y`, `z` of each player, in game metres); the session keeps the last position of each player. For a signed-in caller `GET /catalog/games` gives each client `mapX` and `mapY`: its `x` and `z` as percent (two decimals, kept within 0 to 100 so a player off the map stays on its edge) of the reserve's map picture, with the box and rounding of the launch map's starts ([the launch map](#the-launch-map): world x to the right, z down), or null before the host's first heartbeat with its position (a player who just joined). A session shows its positions only to a caller `Games.Why` lets in (`joinable`) or who is in it: to any other caller, whatever the reason (a friends-only session of a stranger, a hunterscore out of its range, a full session, a session with a password), `mapX`, `mapY`, `x`, `y` and `z` are null, so the positions do not leak through the raw coordinates either. The game's own `Network/list` takes no token and no cookie, so it cannot tell who asks: it sends `x`, `y` and `z` as 0 (a player with no position yet) for the players of a friends-only or password session, and the real ones for any other.

**The host's setup.** `GET /catalog/games/<id>/placed` on `web_host` (signed in with the `hr_session` cookie; 401 `signed_out` without one) answers `{items: [{id, reserve, kind, name, x, y}]}` as [`GET /account/placed`](#the-launch-map) does, but with the **host's** placed items, only those in the session's reserve, and only to a caller `Games.Why` lets in (the rule of `Launch/join`, the same function the list's `joinable` and the positions above use, so the three cannot drift). A session with a password needs the password the player entered in the `X-Game-Password` header (percent-encoded, so any character travels; a header, not the address, which proxies log), and the server checks it before it sends one item. A caller `Games.Why` refuses gets 403 `{status: "refused", why}` with the join's reason (`own`, `friends`, `hunterscore`, `starting`, `network`, `password` for none or a wrong one, `full`) and no items; a session that is not open (or gone) is 404 `{status: "gone"}`. The launcher's join map draws these items on the start places of the reserve ([launcher.md](launcher.md#multiplayer)) and says why in the refusal's own text (only `password` is "Wrong password"). **A start at the host's tent:** a player the session lets in may start at one of the host's tents. `Launch/join` (and the page's `POST /auth/launch` with `mp=join`) takes `host_tent=1` with `start` / `tent` the guid of the tent in the host's items (guids are per account, so the flag says whose); the host's items are read before the session lock is taken, and `Games.Join` reports `bad_start` after the rule of `Games.Why` (a refused player gets that refusal, not `bad_start`, and learns nothing of the host's items) when it is not a tent of the host placed in the session's reserve, and nobody joins. The hunt is recorded at the reserve's first location with no tent of the joiner's own (as a tent start is, with no `Start at` mission objective), the session keeps the guid with the player, and the joiner's connect reads the tent's place and direction from the **host's** item, so the game spawns the joiner at the host's tent; a tent the host picked up since is no start (the connect then starts at the reserve's first location, as for a picked-up tent of one's own). Any other host item (a stand, a caller ...) is only a marker, and another player's tent is never a start. Without `host_tent` the `tent` is the joiner's own tent in that reserve, as for a single-player start; the join map does not offer those any more (its tent markers are the host's, and the two can share a guid).

**The connect reply.** The `session` of `start_mp` or `join` is the token the game connects with (`X-Hun-Session`), and its `network` says what the player is. Any other connect, a single-player hunt among them, keeps `"singleplayer": true` and sends no `ip`.

- **Host:** `{"singleplayer": false, "host": H, "user_id": H, "handle", "ip", "max_players": N, "clients": null}`. `ip` is the address the connect came from.
- **Joiner:** the same with `user_id` the joiner's and `"clients": [{"user_id": H, "handle", "ip", "port", "guid"}]`, the host as the one client: its `ip` is the address of the host's connect, `port` and `guid` (a string) its heartbeat event 29's. A joiner's game connects to that address directly, or through the punch-through server first.
- **Address:** the peer's, or from a `trusted_proxies` peer the last address of `X-Forwarded-For` ([Behind a reverse proxy](#behind-a-reverse-proxy)). A host and a joiner on one LAN must reach the server by its LAN address (the launcher's `server_url`), so that the host's connect is seen from its LAN address and not from loopback. An IPv4-mapped IPv6 peer (`::ffff:a.b.c.d`) is recorded as IPv4. The launcher's forwarder connects to the server over IPv4 first, so a host that plays through it has an IPv4 address whenever the server has one and the host's PC can reach it; an IPv6 address (an older launcher, a PC with no IPv4 route, another tool, or a proxy that connects over IPv6) is sent as the server saw it, but the game's networking is IPv4 only and cannot use it, so such a host's session is listed as not joinable (`why` `network`) and `Launch/join` refuses it.
- **Punch-through:** `config.nat.pt_server` of the connect template is `nat_host` (else `web_host`) on UDP 10000 (`Install.Connect`), where the `natpt` container runs ([server/natpt](../server/natpt/), [deploy.md](deploy.md#punch-through-server)): a RakNet punch-through server, so a joiner behind a home router reaches a host without a forwarded port. It is never the game host's name, which the player kit maps to loopback. The name must resolve, because one that does not hangs the game's start; with nothing answering, the game waits about 30 s, then joins directly (UPnP or a forwarded port). The game has no relay: strict NATs may still fail. A recorded connect reply of a community server gets the same address.

<a id="how-far-a-host-is"></a>**How far a host is.** Each session of `GET /catalog/games` (the launcher's list; the game's own `Network/list` has no such field and is byte for byte as before) has `distance`: `short` (under 3,000 km), `medium` (3,000 km to under 7,000 km) or `long` (7,000 km and more), the great-circle distance (haversine, a 6,371 km sphere) between the approximate place of the **viewer's** request address and of the **host's** connect address (`Member.Ip`), looked up in the `geoip_file` database (`Places`, `Mmdb`, `HostDistance`; the bounds are the constants `HostDistance.Medium` and `HostDistance.Long`). It is null when it is not known: no `geoip_file` or no readable file, an address the file lacks, a private, loopback, link-local or shared (`100.64.0.0/10`) address on either side, no address (a trusted proxy that names none), the caller's own game, and a caller who is not signed in (401). A viewer's address is the one `ClientIp` gives ([Behind a reverse proxy](#behind-a-reverse-proxy)), so the launcher, which reads the list on the public domain, is placed where it connects from; a list read through a server-side call would be placed at that server and show no dot. The launcher reads the word as `GameServer.Distance` and draws the dot, its legend and the credit the database's licence asks for ([launcher.md](launcher.md#multiplayer)); an older server sends no field, which it reads as no dot.

The file is read in place and held open for the server's life (no copy in memory); a replaced file is read at the next start (replace it with `mv`, never by writing into the open file). Looking up an address is a few dozen small positional reads (up to 32 tree nodes for an IPv4 address, 128 for IPv6, then the values of the address's whole record), once per address: the place of an address is remembered in memory, at most 4,096 addresses, and the whole memory is forgotten again 3,570 s (an hour less one sweep) after it was last emptied, at the next lookup or the server's 30-second sweep (so an idle server forgets too, and no place is older than an hour; it is emptied at once when the dots turn off, and at a restart), so one list costs one lookup per new host address and one for the viewer. One lookup decodes at most 10,000 values and reads at most 64 KiB of them (a real record is about a hundred small values). **A damaged file never breaks the list:** a lookup that fails (a file damaged in the middle, overwritten in place, a record that fans out) turns the dots off for the rest of the process's life, prints one line (`distance dots: off (<reason>)`, never an address) and every `distance` is null from then on; a restart reads the file again. At its start the server prints `distance dots: from <file name> (built <date of the file's metadata>)` when the file is in use, else `distance dots: off (<reason>)`. **What is and is not kept:** the addresses and the places they were looked up to are in the server's memory only, for at most an hour, never written to the database, a file or a log (the server logs nothing about a lookup); no address, country, city or coordinate is in any reply, and the list's only trace is the word. A game's distance is given to every signed-in player who asks, also for a game he may not join (friends only, a password), as the original's list gave a continent for every game; the positions of such a game stay hidden. A viewer who asks from many addresses learns no more than which of the three bands around each of them the host's place lies in, never the place itself. The `/your-data` page says so ([website.md](website.md)). The deploy puts the monthly file in place ([deploy.md](deploy.md#the-community-server)).

**The event of a game.** A game runs the seasonal event of its host ([Seasonal events](#seasonal-events)), whoever joins it. Each session of `GET /catalog/games` (the website's and the launcher's list) has `event`, the id of the event the game runs (for example `halloween_2026`; null for none): the event the host's hunt's recorded decision sent (`event_sent`), or while the host has not connected the one the server would decide for him now, when the event lists the game's reserve (with or without content there); on any other reserve it is null, whatever the host's setting. It also has `event_differs`: true when the game's event state differs from what the caller's own single-player hunt on the session's reserve would get now (his setting, the event's window, the reserve; the same rule, so a game on a reserve the event does not list differs from nobody's); false otherwise and for a call with no signed-in player. The game's own `Network/list` has neither field and is unchanged. An older server sends neither field, which the website and the launcher read as null and false. Joining is never refused for it.

**The heartbeat.** Event 29 `{port, guid, color}` stores the player's port (1 to 65535), network id (an unsigned 64-bit number) and colour (6 hex digits). A port or network id that is not valid ignores the event; a colour that is not valid is stored as empty. A host's session can be joined once its event 29 came (the game sends it with its first heartbeat, after the connect). Event 30 `{guid}` (a joiner could not connect to the host) removes the sender from the session when `guid` is the host's. The host's `player_positions` set the members' `x`, `y`, `z`. Every heartbeat keeps its sender in the session.

**The end.** A player's own `disconnect`, a `Launch/start`, `start_single_player` or a tutorial of the same account, a `Launch/start_mp`, a `Launch/join` that is accepted, and 120 s (`HEARTBEAT_TIMEOUT`) of silence after the first heartbeat remove it; a game that sends none is removed after 10 minutes (`Games.Grace`). When the host goes, the session ends and a joiner's next connect is a single-player one.

**Harvests.** In a session any player can harvest any dead animal, but a harvest counts for the player who shot the animal last, and only for that player, except the harvester's harvest objectives (next paragraph). On a harvest (event 3) of a player who is in a session, the server looks at the hits (event 1) on the animal, the same `individual` (and the same species: a hit names it as the catalogue's number plus one, a variant counting as its typical species), that every player of the session made since joining and before the harvest, and takes the newest. Its player is the harvest's: the kill, the harvest page, the score and gm$, the hunterscore, the achievements, the missions and challenges, the taxidermy offer and the trophy are written to that player's account, judged on that player's hits (weapon, ammunition, distance, organ), and the harvester's account records nothing else for it: no event, no gm$ for the kill, no score. The harvester's trophy measurement (event 22) goes along with the harvest, and its spawn record (event 7) stands in for the shooter's own for the score, the harvest page and the harvester's objectives only, when the shooter's events have none; it is not stored as the shooter's event. The shooter's running hunt gets the gm$ (update 15) with its next heartbeat, but no update 6: its game never opened a confirm-kill screen for that animal, and an update 6's `kill_id` becomes the game's taxidermy target, so a screen of the shooter's own harvest open at that moment would have bought its mount for this kill. The harvester's heartbeat reply instead gets the update 6 his confirm-kill screen waits for (without one it stays on "Calculating score" and gives up): the shooter's score, score text, score type and trophy shot from the same judgement, on the harvester's `individual`, with `css` 0 (the screen's reward: 0 gm$) and `kill_id` 0. The mount is the shooter's, and nothing the game hands the page tells the harvester's screen apart from an own harvest, so the page asks the server: the in-game page's script (`/static/app/<version>/client.js`, rewritten as it is served, like the round timer's reads) keeps the confirm-kill page's Taxidermize button disabled until `GET /v1/harvest/screen` (with the player's token) answers, and enables it unless the answer is `{"taxidermy": false}`, which it is when the player's latest harvest counts for another member (kept in memory, `State.HarvestScreenOwn`; the next harvest replaces it). A failed call enables the button, as before; a purchase for kill 0 is refused (404) in any case. The script edit is matched by pattern; if it is not found exactly once it is skipped and one warning is logged. The search for the last hit has no time limit, as in the original ("the last person to hit it before it died"): it looks back to when each player joined. A harvest of the animal by a player of the session since that hit ends it: this one is a second harvest of the same animal (below), unless the spawn records (event 7: species, gender, texture, weight, population, special, feature) of that earlier harvest and of this one both exist and differ; then the earlier harvest was of another animal that had the same `individual` number, the hits before it were on that animal, and this one is an animal nobody in the session hit. A harvest of an animal nobody in the session hit, and one whose newest hit is the harvester's own, is the harvester's as in a single-player hunt (which is unchanged). A second harvest of an animal a player of the session already harvested since its last hit (the game lets two players skin it) is dropped: it counts for nobody, objectives included. The shooter's rows are written in the harvester's heartbeat transaction, so a crash loses neither; when writing them fails the write is undone and logged, and the harvest stays the harvester's (nothing of the heartbeat, its queued updates included, is lost). Known limits: an animal that reuses the number of one harvested since the last hit counts as that animal when a spawn record is missing or both agree (its harvest is then dropped); the shooter's hunterscore follows after the commit, so it can lag one heartbeat if that step fails; two players who hit one animal in the same second may swap which of the two is last; the rule reads the stored events of the session's players only, so a player who never sent a hit (a bow shot that missed, a harvest of an animal found dead) gets nothing; a challenge or achievement the harvester earned before this rule stays earned.

**The harvester's harvest objectives.** A harvest that counts for the shooter also counts for the harvester's mission objectives that ask for a harvest, as the community's mentoring used it (a player with the weapon a mission asks for shoots, another one harvests and gets the objective): in the same transaction (`State.HarvesterEvent`), the harvester's active missions get progress on the objectives whose title's first word (after `Then`, `At any point ...` and the like, [Progress](#missions)) is `Harvest`, judged as the shooter's are: on the shooter's hits (weapon, ammunition, distance, organ, stance, stand, sight, the place the shooter was in) and on the animal (species, gender, coat, weight, harvest value, score). The animal is the shooter's spawn record of it; when the shooter's events have none, the harvester's own spawn record stands in, as it does for the score, so the harvester's gender, coat or weight objective can be met where the shooter's own, which reads the shooter's events only, is not. `Take` and `Kill` titles, which the missions also read as a harvest, do not count for the harvester, nor does a title the operator's [rules](#missions) make a harvest, nor an objective with a sequence (several harvests within a time, one after another, of the same shot or of different animals: those count the harvester's own harvests); spot, clue, arrival and start objectives are the harvester's own events as always. The harvester's daily mission, challenges and community missions do not count it: in the original a daily mission counted only the player's own kills. A mission the harvester completes this way pays its reward to the harvester: the balance (update 15) is added to the reply once the shooter's write and the harvester's objectives are saved (when that write fails, nothing of either reaches the reply), the toasts come with the next heartbeat; the shooter's progress is unchanged. An objective finished this way is `kept` in its row, as a restored one is: no stored event of the harvester's meets it, so the [rollback](#missions), the audit and the stuck report leave it alone. `admin mp-harvests` (below) does not apply this to the harvests it moves: it is the one-off cleanup of the harvests stored before the rule of the paragraph above, and a harvest it moves takes along the harvester's objectives it finished, `Harvest ...` ones included.

**The harvester's trophy shot.** Only the harvester's game takes the trophy shot (event 4, `photo_type` 2), after the harvest, as in the original. When the harvest counted for the shooter, the server notes it on the harvester's side in the same transaction (`harvest_links`: the animal's `individual` and the harvester's latest event 7 of it, the shooter and his new kill), and the first trophy shot the harvester's game asks for of that animal (the same `individual` and latest event 7) becomes the shooter's: it is his kill's picture, in his gallery with the kill's species and score, and in the harvester's gallery as a link to that kill, with the shooter's name ([Gallery](#gallery)). That shot uses the note up: a later trophy shot of the animal, including one after the shooter deleted the first, is the harvester's own, so the harvester cannot keep adding pictures to the shooter's kill. A camera photo (`photo_type` 1) stays the harvester's. A harvest of the harvester's own, and every single-player one, is unchanged. `admin mp-harvests` does not move the photos of the harvests it moves.

`admin mp-harvests [--apply]` moves the harvests stored before this rule to the player who shot the animal last. The sessions are not kept after they end, so it matches the other accounts' events: for each stored harvest, a hit of another account on the same `individual` (a hit names its animal's species as the catalogue's number plus one, a variant counting as its typical species) in the ten minutes before it, in a hunt of the same reserve that overlapped the harvester's and was still running at the harvest, both hunts holding a network event (29: only a game of several players sends it), newer than the harvester's own last hit on it, and both players' spawn records of the animal (event 7: species, gender, texture, weight, population, special and feature, whichever both carry) agree, since an `individual` number is reused by other animals. A harvest with no such hit, or whose newest hit is the harvester's own, is not touched, and neither is a second harvest of an animal (the first one moves; the total counts the ones kept). A moved harvest takes its page (a taxidermy or skull bought with it moves with it, nothing is refunded), its kill row and its events to the shooter under a new kill id of his, the trophy mounted in the harvester's lodge is taken down, and the score is judged again on the shooter's hits. A move is one transaction over both accounts: it is undone whole when any step fails (the line says `failed to move ...` and the total counts it), and the others go on. A new kill id is never one a gm$ payment row of the account names, so the harvester's next harvest does not reuse the id of a moved one. **The gm$ are not moved**: what the harvester was paid stays his, the shooter gets none for it (a wallet row of 0 keeps `admin rescore` from paying it), so the harvest itself changes no wallet; the shooter's missions that are checked again may finish and pay their own rewards as usual. The shooter's missions and challenges are checked again (the confirmation of [Missions](#missions)), the harvester's missions lose only the objectives that harvest finished and no other stored event meets (the rollback of [Missions](#missions), limited to that harvest: an objective that no event meets anyway is not its business), a completed challenge of the harvester stays completed, and the hunterscore of both is made again. It prints one line per harvest (kill, species, from, to, gm$), under it one line per mission objective it reopens and per mission it activates so that nobody is locked out (the dry run lists them too; both are in the record), and a total, and keeps a record per account and move in `mission_rollbacks`. Known limits: the match is by the other accounts' events and times, not by a kept game, so a player who left and rejoined the reserve within the ten minutes can be matched wrongly (the spawn records must agree); a hit by an account that never sent a network event is not matched; a taxidermy bought with a moved harvest stays paid by the harvester and nothing is refunded. Without `--apply` it writes nothing. Run it once after the deploy, with the server stopped, after a [backup](deploy.md#backups), like `admin missions-rollback`.

## Licenses

The original sold a license to hunt a species (1, 7 or 28 days) and licenses of the reserves; the catalogue's item type of them is `LICENSE` (the facts file's `types`, number 13; their slot is the lodge's, 0). With `all_licenses` on (the default) every player has all of them, without end, until the operator sets it to `false`:

- **Connect:** every entry of `spawn_licenses` other than 2 becomes 1 (licensed), so the game shows no "need license" notice and refuses no kill; an entry of 2 (the species is not available) stays 2. The admin spawner still sets its own species to 1.
- **Inventory:** the connect reply's `inventory.items` lists one lodge-slot row (no units) of every license item the catalogue or the install's definitions have, with its definition in `infos`, in place of the account's own license rows; `GET /account/inventory` lists the same rows with `expires` 0, which the store shows as "You have an unlimited license" with no buy button.
- **Buying:** `Inventory/buy` and `POST /account/buy` refuse a license as already owned (`{"success": false}`, 409 `refused`), nothing is charged. A bundle that lists one is not for sale: `buyable` is false in `GET /catalog/store` (the website shows its buy button disabled) and `POST /account/buy` refuses it.

With it off, a license is an item like any other: sold, owned by the rows the account has, its `spawn_licenses` as the connect carries it.

## Harvest gm$ bonus

With `harvest_gm_bonus` set, a harvest made from the `from` of a bonus until its `until` (the server's clock at the heartbeat that carries the harvest; `from` included, `until` not) has its CSS multiplied by that bonus's `multiplier`, rounded to one decimal, before anything uses it. A harvest outside every period pays ×1:

- **Update 6** carries the multiplied `css`. The game builds the confirm-kill screen's "Reward: X gm$" from it (`round(css)`), and the chat line's CSS too, so the player sees the bonus.
- **The payment** is `round` of that CSS, under the usual `CSS Reward (Animal Id: <kill>)` label, so a rescore never pays it again.
- **Its base and event.** The payment's `wallet_log` row also keeps `base`, the gm$ the harvest would have paid without the bonus (`round` of the unmultiplied CSS), and `bonus_event`, the id of the bonus's row in the events log ([Reward bases and events](#reward-bases-and-events)); outside a bonus `base` is the amount and `bonus_event` is empty.
- The score, the leaderboards, the Hall of Fame and the score sheets use the score, not the CSS: unchanged. Missions, daily missions and dog rewards are not multiplied.

After `until` a bonus stops by itself; the setting can stay until the operator tidies it.

**Scheduling without a restart.** Write every bonus in the list, the one running and the ones to come, for example the ×1.25 that runs now and the ×2 weekend that starts where it ends:

```json
"harvest_gm_bonus": [
  {"multiplier": 1.25, "until": "2026-11-27T15:00:00Z"},
  {"multiplier": 2, "from": "2026-11-27T15:00:00Z", "until": "2026-11-30T07:00:00Z", "name": "Harvest gm$ x2 weekend"}
]
```

The server looks at `settings.json` every 5 seconds and, when it changed, reads `harvest_gm_bonus`, `store_sales` and `referral` again (only those three settings: the others are read at start; the [Store sales](#store-sales) and the [Referrals](#referrals) use the same look). A good list replaces the one in force at once, and each bonus not yet over is written to the events log then. A list that is wrong (not JSON, a bad value, overlapping periods) is refused: the log says `harvest_gm_bonus: not applied, the bonuses in force stay: <why>` (`store_sales: not applied, the sales in force stay: <why>` for a wrong sale, `referral: not applied, the referral settings in force stay: <why>` for a wrong referral block; a good setting beside a wrong one is still applied; a file that is not JSON or not an object: `settings: not applied, the bonuses and sales in force stay: <why>`) and the previous bonuses go on (a read that fails for another reason, such as a file held by an editor, is logged and tried again at the next look). Nothing else is needed: no restart, no job. The file is polled, not watched, because the server's container has the folder bind-mounted ([deploy.md](deploy.md)) and an editor that replaces the file or a mount that carries no change notices would blind a file watcher, while a poll of the file's time and size sees every case. A bonus with no `from` (today's `{"multiplier", "until"}`) starts when it is read, so write the start of one that begins later.

## Store sales

`store_sales` of `settings.json` is the store's only way to lower a price: a list of sales the operator writes, read at start and again within 5 seconds of every change of the file, without a restart ([the same look as the gm$ bonus](#harvest-gm-bonus)). Nothing is on sale until a sale is written (the shipped `settings.json` has none). **The recorded `Application/discounts` of the original is not read any more**: `was`, `gwas` and `sale` of `GET /catalog/store` are null everywhere without a sale, and `Application/discounts` (the game's store) answers `[]`.

```json
"store_sales": [
  {"name": "Black Friday", "from": "2026-11-27T15:00:00Z", "until": "2026-11-30T07:00:00Z",
   "percent": 25, "currencies": ["em", "gm"], "items": [123, 456], "bundles": [9]},
  {"name": "Daily deals", "event": "halloween_2026",
   "random": {"count": 8, "percent": [10, 40], "every": "24h",
              "categories": ["weapons", "ammo"], "min_price_em": 50, "exclude": [791]}}
]
```

- **`name`** is the sale's name, shown on the item (`sale.name`) and unique (case does not count). **`from`** and **`until`** are ISO times (no zone means UTC), `from` before `until`; both are required unless `event` is given.
- **A fixed sale** has `percent` (a whole number above 0 and below 100: 12.5 is refused) and `items` (item ids) and/or `bundles` (bundle ids), at least one id. **A random sale** has `random` instead (items only, never bundles) and no `percent` outside it.
- **`currencies`**: `["em"]` (the default), `["gm"]` or `["em", "gm"]`: which prices of the sale's items drop. A bundle's gm$ price is 17 × its em$ price ([The gm$ rate](#the-store-catalogue)), so a bundle of a sale whose currencies are `["gm"]` has only that price lowered, and one whose currencies are `["em"]` only its em$ price; an item's gm$ price without a `silver_price` in the catalogue is that derived price too.
- **`event`**: the name of a seasonal event (`event.name` of the settings, [Seasonal events](#seasonal-events)) or of a harvest gm$ bonus (its `name`, else `Harvest gm$ x<multiplier>`, [Harvest gm$ bonus](#harvest-gm-bonus)), case does not count. The sale runs only while that event runs, inside its own `from`/`until` when it has them; with none, it takes the event's. An event that does not exist or has not been set: the sale never runs, and the log says `store_sales: <name>: event <x> unknown, the sale never runs` once at each start and reload (a bonus that is added later makes it run, but is not logged again). A bonus that is scheduled several times under one name gives the sale each of its periods.
- **`random`** picks `count` (1 to 1000) of the items the store sells for a currency of the sale and that match the filters: `categories` (names: the store key of the sub-category, its title or its category's title, case does not count; none: every category), `min_price_em` and `max_price_em` (the catalogue's em$ price), `min_price_gm` and `max_price_gm` (its gm$ price), `exclude` (item ids). An item the store does not sell (hidden, not buyable, a service, no price in the sale's currencies, an unlimited ammunition or fuel) is never picked. Each pick has its own percentage: `percent` is one number, or `[lowest, highest]`, and the percentage is a multiple of 5 in that range (so the range holds one). `every` (`"90m"`, `"24h"`, `"7d"`) picks again at every such step from the start of the run (the sale's own period cut by the event's); without it, one pick holds for the whole run. Fewer items than `count` match: fewer are picked.
- **The pick is reproducible.** The matching items are put in the order of a SHA-256 of the sale's name, the period's start (Unix seconds) and the item id, the first `count` are kept, and the percentage of each comes from another hash of the same: there is no random number generator, so a restart or a reload of the setting gives the same items for the same period, and every player sees the same deals. The pick is worked out again from the sale and the catalogue at every start and reload, so a changed filter or catalogue changes the pick of the running period too. **The picks are written down**: at every look of the file (5 seconds) the server writes the pick of the current and of the next period of each random sale to the table `store_sale_picks` (`sale`, `period_start`, `period_end`, `item_id`, `percent`; schema 45, created at every start), once per period: a period already in the table is left as it was, so the history is the first pick of each period.
- **Two sales on one item**: the larger discount applies, never both (a gm$ sale and an em$ sale on one item each lower their own currency). The `sale` field is the one of the larger percentage.
- **The price rule.** A sale price is `price − price × percent / 100` rounded half up to a whole em$ / gm$, and at least 1 (a sale never makes an item free). The percentage is taken off the catalogue's list price, never off another sale's price. For a **bundle** the sale comes first: the sale's percentage is taken off the bundle's list price, and then [the share of the items the player already owns](#bundles) is taken off that price (`320 x 250 / 360` for a bundle of 400 at 20 % with 250 of 360 left to give, and not `400 x 250 / 360` and then 20 %).
- **Where it shows.** `GET /catalog/store` (`ems` and `was`, `gms` and `gwas`, and `sale: {name, percent, until}`, `until` being the end of the sale's current period: the earliest of the sale's `until`, its event's end and the end of the current random period; null without a sale), the bundles of it (`price`, `was`, `gms`, `gwas`, `sale`), `GET /account/bundles`, `Inventory/buy` (the game and the launcher), `POST /account/buy` (the website) and the refill service of the bait sites charge the sale price. The game's store reads `Application/discounts`, which the server answers from the sales: a row `{item_id, percentage, start_ts, end_ts}` for each item whose em$ price is lowered now (the original's discounts are em$ only: **a gm$ sale shows in the website and the launcher's store but not in the game's own store**, which still charges the sale gm$ price at the purchase). `Store/products2` is still the recording's.
- **A wrong sale** (a percentage out of range, `random` and `items` together, a bad time, a duplicate name) is refused as a whole list: the sales in force stay and the log says why.

**Admin.** `dotnet run --project server/Server -- admin sales` reads `settings.json` and lists every sale, then its current period and its next one with the items (and bundles) on sale in it and what each costs then (`em$ 130 -> 98`): the picks of a random sale can be read before it starts. It writes nothing.

## Unlimited ammunition

The original's unlimited-ammo service gave a player every ammunition without limit. With `unlimited_ammo` on (the default) every player has it, until the operator sets it to `false`:

- **Connect:** every connect reply (single player, multiplayer and tutorial) has `inventory.active_services.UNLIMITED_AMMO` set to the integer 1, which switches the game's mode on: the lodge and tent inventory show every ammunition with an infinity badge. `UNLIMITED_FUEL` follows `unlimited_fuel` ([Unlimited fuel](#unlimited-fuel)); the other services (`STREAK_PROTECTION`) stay as the connect has them.
- **Hunt start:** every launch ([The free ammunition](#starting-data)) first decides which lodge stacks the game is shown: of the lodge's stacks of one ammunition (older free boxes, a restore) only the oldest; the others stay in the database, marked hidden (metadata key `__hidden`), and are left out of the connect, `Inventory/list` and `Inventory/dict` (`GET /admin/inventory` lists them), and a request naming one (a slot in `Inventory/move_multiple2`'s `mapping`, an event 6, 8, 23, 25 or 33) changes nothing. Nothing is deleted: the launch clears the previous marks before its loadout is put on (the loadout may take any box) and sets them again after it, so with the setting off they are the game's again at the next launch (the website's inventory lists every row). It then fills every ammunition stack the account owns (catalogue category 3, with units), of any type, to a full box in the launch's transaction, before the connect; a larger stack is never lowered. The free ammunition is topped up to one box instead of `boxes`.
- **Ammunition of the weapons the player owns:** as ammunition is not sold, the same launch gives the account one full box, a row of its own listed in the connect that follows, of every ammunition that fits a weapon it owns and has no row yet, so no weapon is without ammunition in the lodge. What fits a weapon is the `fit` list of the ammunition category in the catalogue, or, when the server has no recorded catalogue (or the list is empty), the ammunition among the attachments of the weapon's install definition: the rule never depends on `api_host` or the recordings. An ammunition the game has no definition of (neither a recorded one nor the install's ballistics) is left out: the game closes after the connect for an item it cannot define. An equipped weapon (slots 2 to 4) whose ammunition slot (the weapon's slot plus 6, so 8 to 10) is empty gets a copy of its first fitting ammunition in that slot, marked as a copy like a box the page copied ([Boxes at a lodge or tent](#unlimited-ammunition) below), so the lodge keeps its own stack: a weapon raised with an empty ammunition slot closes the game at the first shot ([#608](https://github.com/yinyin99/hunter-resurgence/issues/608)). A weapon whose slot has an ammunition is left alone.
- **One lodge stack of every ammunition:** last, the launch gives the lodge (slot 0) a full box of every ammunition the account owns that has no row there, also when its only box is equipped or in the backpack: the page lists and copies only what the lodge holds, and copies a stack only when it is the only one there, so the lodge shows each ammunition once as the infinite stack. A launch adds it once, marked as a copy, so the launch after the player put another box of it back into the lodge removes it again; a stack the player already has in the lodge is never duplicated.
- **Boxes at a lodge or tent:** the infinity badge stays on the lodge's stack and the page copies it: dragging it into the backpack or choosing it for a weapon's ammunition slot leaves the stack in the lodge and puts a copy under a placeholder guid (`new_item_<ms>`) where the player dropped it (the page copies only while the lodge holds exactly one stack of that ammunition; while it holds more, a drag moves one of them, until one is left). When the inventory closes or a tab changes, the page sends `Inventory/move_multiple2` with the placeholders in `clonedItems` and every item's slot in `mapping`, and expects `{"<placeholder>": <integer guid>}` (or `null`) back. It then gives each copy that guid, tells the game about the stack (item, guid, slot, units) and only after that updates the game's slots with every item's guid; a guid the game was never told about empties the slot it is put in, and a shot with an empty ammunition slot closes the game ([#608](https://github.com/yinyin99/hunter-resurgence/issues/608)). So each entry creates a stack of one full box, the catalogue's stack size whatever the request says, as a row of the account with a new integer guid (the account's highest plus 1, never above 2^31 - 1, a number the game can hold), and the reply maps each placeholder this request created to its guid; the next connect has the rows. Only a catalogue ammunition the account already owns (with a stack size) is created, and not past 100 rows of one ammunition: at 100 the oldest hidden lodge stack of it (a launch hides the extra lodge stacks) becomes the box instead, shown again and full, and its guid is the answer, so the page always gets a number (with only its placeholder the game is given a text guid and the inventory never resumes the game); with none hidden nothing is created. An entry for any other item, a name that is no placeholder, or while unlimited ammunition is off, creates nothing. A placeholder that already has its box (a tab change and a close can overlap and the page asks twice) creates nothing and is not in the reply, because the page replaced the placeholder when it got the first answer and stops at a key it no longer knows; the entry's slot in `mapping` still applies to that box. The account's copies and its row count per item are read once per request, and only when it names a placeholder, so a request of any size is one pass under the write lock. A tutorial creates nothing and always answers `null` (its item numbers are not the account's). A copy is marked in its row (metadata key `__clone`, never shown to the game, in `Inventory/list` or `Inventory/dict`), and a launch removes a marked box in the lodge when an older row of the same ammunition is in the lodge too, so boxes taken out and put back do not pile up; a copy in the backpack or in a weapon stays, and the launch fill above fills it like any stack. A loadout's extra boxes at a launch (rows of the connect, which the game knows) are unchanged.
- **Refill by heartbeat:** the refill the game sends by event 6 with a negative `quantity` (used ammunition put back) stops at the item's stack size and never lowers a larger stack; an item with no stack size is not refilled by it. This holds whatever the setting is.
- **Buying:** `Inventory/buy` and `POST /account/buy` refuse an ammunition like a license under `all_licenses` (`{"success": false}`, 409 `refused`), nothing is charged; a bundle that lists one is refused as a whole. In `GET /catalog/store` such an item is `unlimited` and not `buyable`, and the website shows "Unlimited ammunition" and no buy button ([the store catalogue](#the-store-catalogue)). A grant by the admin API is not a purchase and still works.

With it off, ammunition is an item like any other: sold, used up shot by shot, and only the free ammunition is refilled at a launch. (A recorded connect that already carries the service keeps the loadout's extra boxes and the copies on, as before; the launch steps above follow the setting only, so such a server neither fills an equipped weapon's empty ammunition slot nor adds the lodge stacks.)

## Ownership limits

A player owns at most as many copies of an item as he can use: one of each weapon, binoculars, clothing piece, upgrade or trophy-shot pose, several of what he can use at once (a scope for each weapon, placeable stands and blinds, consumables). One table decides it, the facts file's `own_limits` ([The facts file](#the-facts-file)), and one function applies it (`State.OwnLimit`: the item's own entry, else its `client_type`'s, then the catalogue's `ownLimit` as a cap); a copy is one inventory row.

| `client_type` | Most a player owns |
|---|---|
| 1, 22 (rifles, shotguns, handguns) | 1 of each |
| 0, 4 (binoculars, rangefinders, camera, callers, scents, baits, consumables) | the catalogue's: 1 when it marks the item `ownMultiple: false` (an item with `units` is exempt), no limit when it marks it multiple; a catalogue built from the install alone has no such mark, so there an item that counts uses (a positive `units`: scents, face paint, a water bottle) is multiple and the others (binoculars, callers, the horn) are 1 |
| 43 (Electronic Caller add-ons) | 1 of each |
| 7 to 11, 34, 35, 36 (clothing, glasses, scarves, backpacks) | 1 of each |
| 15, 21, 39 (upgrades, pouches, holster, lanyard) | 1 |
| 31, 40, 24, 6 (trophy-shot poses, styles, gun racks, avatars, minigame) | 1 of each |
| 42 (vehicles) | 1 of each |
| 26, 27, 29, 30 (dog collars, vests, GPS, backpacks) | 1 of each |
| 2, 3, 25, 12, 32, 14, 28, 38 (scopes, ammunition, dogs, placeables, lodges, bait refills, camping supplies, treats) | no limit |

Two items override their type: the Hunting Horn (619) is limited to 1 and the Electronic Caller (792, a placeable) is not limited. A catalogue `ownLimit` still caps whatever the table says. Every path that puts an item into an inventory goes through it:

- **Buying:** the store pages, `Inventory/buy` and `POST /account/buy` refuse a purchase that would put the player over the limit (`{"success": false}` and 409 `refused`, nothing charged) and the website greys the button of an item whose limit is reached (`limit` of `GET /catalog/store`, [Buying from the website](#buying-from-the-website)).
- **Restores and claims:** a backup zip or an archive claim brings at most the limit of each item: the copies in use stay (equipped, in a weapon or scope slot, placed in the world), then the oldest guid; the report says how many were not restored. The start items a restore adds are one of each, as before.
- **The start kit:** the starting profile (and a recorded connect used as one) is cut the same way.
- **Admin grants:** `admin item`, `POST /admin/grants/items` grant nothing (409 `Own limit` for the API) when the account would own more than the limit.
- **Not limited by this rule:** ammunition (unlimited ammunition makes its own boxes), licenses (their end is in the row) and fuel; a lodge item is not limited either, one lodge is added per unit.

### Bundles

A bundle gives at most the limit of each item it lists, whatever it lists (one weapon, one clothing piece, ...), and not again an item the player owns up to its limit. Its price drops by the share of those items: each item's own em$ list price (the catalogue's, whatever sale is on) over the sum of those of all the items it lists, `price × (1 − owned share)`, rounded up to a whole em$ and at least 1; `price` is the bundle's price under a [sale](#store-sales) that lists it, so the sale's percentage is taken off first and the share second. A bundle of 5 items at 60 em$ with 2 items owned that are worth a third of the 5 costs 40 em$ and gives the other 3. A bundle that would give nothing is not sold (its list price stays, the sale's while one is on). The server works the price out itself, inside the purchase's transaction, whatever the page showed. `GET /account/bundles` (the session cookie) answers `{bundles: [{id, price, gms, owned, of, buyable}]}` for the bundles `GET /catalog/store` lists (`price` in em$, `gms` the same price in gm$, 17 × the em$ one before the share): the website and the launcher show `price` and `gms` (the list price struck through when it is lower) and "You own `owned` of `of` items" (with ": `price` em$ instead of the list price" only when the price dropped), or that the player owns all of them. A copy a bundle lists beyond the item's limit (the same weapon twice) is not given, not paid for and not counted in `of`. A bundle made only of ammunition while `unlimited_ammo` is on, and one that lists a fuel while `unlimited_fuel` is on, are left out of the store instead of shown as not buyable; they come back when the setting is off.

### The pass at start

The inventories made before the rule hold more than the limit (restores and the start kit never checked it). Once, at the first start of a community server (`offline_mode` false) that has it, after the lodge backfill, `State.EnforceOwnLimits` goes through every account: for each item over its limit it keeps up to the limit of the copies in use first (equipped, in a weapon or scope slot, placed in the world, worn by a dog: any slot above 0, or a position), then the oldest, and removes the rest. When more copies are in use than the limit (one weapon in two weapon slots), the rest goes even if equipped. Each account is one transaction, with **no refund**. Ammunition, licenses, fuel and every item with no limit are never touched. Each removed row is kept whole in `own_limit_removals` (`id, account_id, ts, guid, item_id, slot, metadata, units`, schema 38, created at every start if missing), and one line per account that lost copies is printed at start. The pass runs once: when it has finished it writes a row in `own_limit_pass` (shared, schema 38, created at every start if missing), and a later start removes nothing, so a tighter `own_limits` or another catalogue never deletes a copy at a later start. Neither the server nor the deploy script takes a backup: the operator copies the database before the deploy that brings it ([docs/deploy.md](deploy.md#the-community-server), a manual step), and a copy removed by mistake is put back from `own_limit_removals`.

## Unlimited fuel

The original's unlimited-fuel service made a jeep never use up its fuel. With `unlimited_fuel` on (the default) every player has it, until the operator sets it to `false`:

- **Connect:** every connect reply has `inventory.active_services.UNLIMITED_FUEL` set to the integer 1, whatever the recorded connect had (the other services stay as it has them). Whether the jeep then drives without fuel items and with a full gauge is to be checked in the game.
- **Buying:** `Vehicle Fuel - 25 / 50 / 100 Units` (items 953, 954 and 955) are refused by `Inventory/buy` and `POST /account/buy` (`{"success": false}`, 409 `refused`), nothing is charged; in `GET /catalog/store` they have `fuel` true and `buyable` false and the website shows "Unlimited fuel" and no buy button; a bundle that lists one is left out of the store and refused. A grant by the admin API still works.
- **Existing fuel rows** stay (harmless), and the pass at start does not touch them.

With it off, fuel is an item like any other.

## Buying from the website

`POST /account/buy` on `web_host`, community server only, with the session cookie and the website's `X-Requested-With` (like the other `/account/` calls, [Upload a backup zip](#upload-a-backup-zip)); the website's server and the launcher send it, a browser page never does. The form is `id`, `count` (1 to 1,000, default 1), `wallet` (`GOLD`, the default, pays em$; `SILVER` pays gm$, the names of `Inventory/buy`) and `bundle=1` for a bundle:

- **An item** the store lists as buyable ([The store catalogue](#the-store-catalogue)) costs `count` times today's em$ price (`GOLD`), or `count` times its gm$ price (`SILVER`, the catalogue's `silver_price`, else 17 × the em$ price), each the sale price while a [sale](#store-sales) lowers it. An item with no gm$ price of at least 1 is not sold for gm$, so it is never free.
- **A bundle** is one at a time, at its rounded em$ price (`GOLD`) or its gm$ price (`SILVER`, [The gm$ rate](#the-store-catalogue)), and gives each item it lists (a hidden item too) up to its limit, at the price [Bundles](#bundles) works out for the account; every item must be in the catalogue.

Neither path sells past the ownership limits ([Ownership limits](#ownership-limits): the facts file's table, then the catalogue's `ownLimit` and `ownMultiple`): an item is refused when the player's copies (inventory rows) plus the purchase would pass its limit (a bundle gives each of its items up to its limit instead and is refused only when it would give nothing). With `all_licenses` on, a license item is always over the limit ([Licenses](#licenses)). A one-use item (the catalogue gives it `units`: taxidermy and skull-mount orders, the bait-site refill) is exempt from the `ownMultiple` rule, because the original store never showed one as owned. `Inventory/buy` refuses with `{"success": false}` like a balance too low; the check is the original store's (its `STORE_ITEM_STATE_OWNLIMIT_REACHED`). A catalogue item without those fields has no limit.

The charge and the items are one transaction, the one `Inventory/buy` uses (it takes the same `wallet`): only the wallet paid is debited, the change is a row of that wallet's history (`wallet_log`, summary `In-game payment.`, so the totals spent count it), and a running game gets the items (update 10) and the new balance of that wallet (update 15, `em` or `gm`) with its next heartbeat, else the next connect carries them.
